CAT VARC Chapter 11: Odd Sentence Out

Verbal Ability · 250 questions · Teacher book

Paper Tree Educational Studio · v1.0.0

CAT-VARC-C11-001 · Odd sentence out · MediumTITA
Five jumbled sentences (labelled 1, 2, 3, 4 and 5), related to a topic, are given below. Four of them can be put together to form a coherent paragraph. Identify the odd sentence and key in its number as your answer.

1.Moral philosophers have also asked whether a person can fairly be blamed for desires she never chose and cannot simply switch off.

2.We tend to admire the person who resists a strong temptation to lie more than the person who is never tempted at all.

3.The struggle we admire is evidence of effort, but it is also evidence that the desire to deceive is still present and still strong.

4.Perhaps, then, we should praise struggle as a stage on the way to virtue, while recognising that the goal is a character in which the struggle is no longer needed.

5.Yet on reflection the second person seems the better one, since honesty has become so much a part of her character that lying no longer appeals.

Type your answer
Answer and explanation

Answer: 1

Sentence 2 reports our tendency to admire the person who struggles against temptation, sentence 5 turns ('Yet') to the untempted person as the better one, sentence 3 explains why the struggle is a mixed sign, and sentence 4 reconciles the two ('then') by treating struggle as a stage towards virtue. Sentence 1 uses the paragraph's vocabulary of desire and moral assessment, but it raises a separate question, blame for unchosen desires, which no other sentence takes up; the paragraph is about whom we should admire, not whom we may blame.

Common wrong entry 3: Sentence 3 can look like a restatement, but it supplies the reason for the reversal in sentence 5: the struggle shows the bad desire is still there, which is what sentence 4 builds on.

Common misconception: Treating sentence 1 as part of the discussion because it mentions desires, without noticing that it concerns blame rather than admiration.

Difficulty: The odd sentence shares the moral vocabulary of desire and assessment; it is rejected because it asks a different question from the one the paragraph resolves. · Exams: CAT, XAT, NMAT, SNAP, CMAT, MAH-CET

CAT-VARC-C11-002 · Odd sentence out · EasyTITA
Five jumbled sentences (labelled 1, 2, 3, 4 and 5), related to a topic, are given below. Four of them can be put together to form a coherent paragraph. Identify the odd sentence and key in its number as your answer.

1.Students who cram the night before an exam often perform reasonably well the next morning.

2.Many students also find that background music helps them concentrate, though whether it improves learning remains uncertain.

3.Weeks later, however, much of what they learned has vanished, while material studied in several shorter sessions spread over time is retained far better.

4.The practical lesson is that a revision timetable should revisit topics at widening intervals rather than devote a single long block to each.

5.Psychologists call this the spacing effect, and one explanation is that each return to half-forgotten material forces the effortful recall that strengthens memory.

Type your answer
Answer and explanation

Answer: 2

Sentences 1 and 3 contrast cramming with spaced study, sentence 5 names the result ('call this the spacing effect') and explains it, and sentence 4 draws the lesson for a revision timetable. Sentence 2 is about studying too, but it concerns concentration and background music, which has nothing to do with how study is distributed over time.

Common wrong entry 1: Sentence 1 can seem to praise cramming against the drift of the paragraph, but the short-term success it reports is exactly what 'Weeks later, however' in sentence 3 sets up to overturn.

Common misconception: Choosing a sentence simply because it is about students, without checking that it connects to the timing of study.

Difficulty: The odd sentence moves to a different study habit and has no link to the timing argument. · Exams: CAT, XAT, NMAT, SNAP, CMAT, MAH-CET

CAT-VARC-C11-003 · Odd sentence out · MediumTITA
Five jumbled sentences (labelled 1, 2, 3, 4 and 5), related to a topic, are given below. Four of them can be put together to form a coherent paragraph. Identify the odd sentence and key in its number as your answer.

1.Recipes also record the history of trade, since a dish that calls for pepper or cloves tells us which spices a region could obtain.

2.This is why cooking traditions have survived for generations in kitchens where little was ever written down, and why a cookbook alone rarely makes a cook.

3.Such judgements are learned by watching and tasting beside someone who already has them, not by reading.

4.A written recipe looks like a complete set of instructions, yet two cooks following it exactly can produce very different dishes.

5.The difference lies in what the recipe leaves unsaid: how thick the batter should feel, what the onions should smell like when they are ready, when the heat must drop.

Type your answer
Answer and explanation

Answer: 1

Sentence 4 sets up the puzzle that the same recipe yields different dishes, sentence 5 explains 'The difference' by what recipes leave unsaid, sentence 3 says how 'Such judgements' are acquired, and sentence 2 draws the consequence ('This is why') for how cooking is passed on. Sentence 1 is about recipes, but it treats them as historical evidence of trade, which has nothing to do with the argument that written instructions cannot carry a cook's judgement.

Common wrong entry 4: Sentence 4 may look like a mere observation, but 'The difference' in sentence 5 refers to the different dishes it describes.

Common misconception: Accepting sentence 1 because it is about recipes, without checking that it serves the argument about what recipes fail to transmit.

Difficulty: The odd sentence shares the paragraph's central term and is rejected only on function: it uses recipes as evidence rather than asking what they can teach. · Exams: CAT, XAT, NMAT, SNAP, CMAT, MAH-CET

CAT-VARC-C11-004 · Odd sentence out · MediumTITA
Five jumbled sentences (labelled 1, 2, 3, 4 and 5), related to a topic, are given below. Four of them can be put together to form a coherent paragraph. Identify the odd sentence and key in its number as your answer.

1.Open voting let landlords, employers and party agents see exactly how each person voted, and reward or punish them accordingly.

2.Its effect was to make the vote belong more fully to the voter, whose choice no longer had to answer to those with power over their livelihood.

3.The secret ballot removed that possibility, because a vote that cannot be seen cannot be reliably bought or punished.

4.For much of the history of elections, voters declared their choices in public, by a show of hands or by calling out a name before officials and neighbours.

5.Open voting was also slow, since each voter had to be called forward and the choice recorded by hand before the next could speak.

Type your answer
Answer and explanation

Answer: 5

Sentence 4 describes public voting, sentence 1 shows how it exposed voters to pressure from the powerful, sentence 3 explains how the secret ballot removed 'that possibility', and sentence 2 states 'Its effect' on the voter's independence. Sentence 5 is about open voting too, but it makes a point about slowness, a practical inconvenience that plays no part in an argument about coercion and the voter's freedom.

Common wrong entry 2: Sentence 2 may look like a summary that could be dropped, but it states what the paragraph is building to: the ballot's effect on whom the vote answers to, which links back to the 'landlords, employers and party agents' of sentence 1.

Common misconception: Accepting sentence 5 because it begins with 'Open voting', as sentence 1 does, and adds a further drawback, without asking whether that drawback connects to the secret ballot's purpose.

Difficulty: The odd sentence shares the subject and opens like a coherent sentence; it is rejected because its drawback (delay) is not the one the secret ballot answers (pressure). · Exams: CAT, XAT, NMAT, SNAP, CMAT, MAH-CET

CAT-VARC-C11-005 · Odd sentence out · EasyTITA
Five jumbled sentences (labelled 1, 2, 3, 4 and 5), related to a topic, are given below. Four of them can be put together to form a coherent paragraph. Identify the odd sentence and key in its number as your answer.

1.In some restaurants the bill is presented in a small folder, a convention that lets the diner work out a tip without being watched.

2.If the critics are right, tipping transfers risk from employers to workers without delivering the incentive that is supposed to justify it.

3.Critics reply that tips track the customer's mood, the size of the bill and even the server's appearance more closely than the quality of service.

4.In countries where tipping is customary, a large share of a waiter's income may depend on the generosity of strangers.

5.Defenders of the custom argue that it rewards good service, since diners can tip more when they are well looked after.

Type your answer
Answer and explanation

Answer: 1

Sentence 4 establishes that waiters' income depends on tips, sentence 5 gives the defence of the custom, sentence 3 gives the critics' reply, and sentence 2 draws the conclusion 'If the critics are right', linking the risk of sentence 4 with the failed incentive of sentence 5. Sentence 1 is about tipping, but it describes a point of restaurant etiquette that plays no part in the debate over whether tips reward service.

Common wrong entry 4: Sentence 4 is needed: the 'risk' that sentence 2 says tipping transfers to workers is the dependence of income on strangers that sentence 4 describes.

Common misconception: Choosing sentence 4 because it states a fact rather than an argument, though the conclusion depends on it.

Difficulty: The paragraph is a simple defence and reply; the odd sentence is a piece of etiquette with no argumentative role. · Exams: CAT, XAT, NMAT, SNAP, CMAT, MAH-CET

CAT-VARC-C11-006 · Odd sentence out · MediumTITA
Five jumbled sentences (labelled 1, 2, 3, 4 and 5), related to a topic, are given below. Four of them can be put together to form a coherent paragraph. Identify the odd sentence and key in its number as your answer.

1.Artificial light also hides the stars from many city dwellers, who may go years without seeing the Milky Way.

2.Because insects pollinate plants and feed birds and bats, losses at countless lamps may ripple through whole food webs.

3.Shielding lamps, dimming them late at night or choosing wavelengths that attract fewer insects can reduce this toll without leaving streets dark.

4.A single street lamp can therefore draw insects out of the surrounding habitat night after night, acting as a trap rather than a beacon.

5.Many night-flying insects are drawn to artificial lights and, once there, circle them until they are exhausted or eaten.

Type your answer
Answer and explanation

Answer: 1

Sentence 5 describes how insects are drawn to lights, sentence 4 draws the consequence ('therefore') that a lamp acts as a trap, sentence 2 widens the harm to food webs, and sentence 3 proposes ways to reduce 'this toll'. Sentence 1 is about artificial light at night too, but it concerns what people lose sight of, not what happens to insects, so it connects to none of the steps about insects, food webs or lamp design.

Common wrong entry 2: Sentence 2 can look like a jump from lamps to ecology, but it explains why the tempting error of sentence 4 matters, and the 'toll' of sentence 3 includes these losses.

Common misconception: Grouping every effect of artificial light together and so missing that the paragraph is about one harm, the toll on insects.

Difficulty: The odd sentence shares the theme of light pollution and begins with 'also'; it is rejected because it changes the victim from insects to people. · Exams: CAT, XAT, NMAT, SNAP, CMAT, MAH-CET

CAT-VARC-C11-007 · Odd sentence out · DifficultTITA
Five jumbled sentences (labelled 1, 2, 3, 4 and 5), related to a topic, are given below. Four of them can be put together to form a coherent paragraph. Identify the odd sentence and key in its number as your answer.

1.Most people judge it worse to push someone into a river than to fail to rescue a stranger who has fallen in, even when the outcome is the same.

2.Defenders of the distinction answer that a duty not to harm can be owed to everyone at once, whereas a duty to rescue everyone in danger would consume a whole life.

3.Philosophers also disagree about whether an omission can be a cause at all, since if failing to rescue causes a death, so do the failures of everyone else who might have helped.

4.Some philosophers dismiss this intuition as a bias, arguing that if the consequences are identical, the moral verdicts should be too.

5.On their account the asymmetry is no bias but a recognition that negative duties, unlike positive ones, can be fully honoured by every person simultaneously.

Type your answer
Answer and explanation

Answer: 3

Sentence 1 states the intuition, sentence 4 reports the objection that it is a bias, sentence 2 gives the defenders' answer in terms of what duties can be owed to everyone, and sentence 5 concludes 'On their account' that the asymmetry rests on a difference between negative and positive duties. Sentence 3 shares the vocabulary of omissions and rescue, but it opens a separate metaphysical question about causation; neither the objection nor the reply turns on whether omissions cause anything, so it attaches to nothing.

Common wrong entry 4: Sentence 4 can look like an interruption, but the defenders in sentence 2 'answer' it, and sentence 5 denies precisely its charge that the asymmetry is a bias.
Common wrong entry 5: Sentence 5 may seem to repeat sentence 2, but it turns the defenders' point into the conclusion that answers sentence 4: the asymmetry is 'no bias'.

Common misconception: Assuming that any point about omissions must support the defenders, when their reply rests entirely on the structure of duties, not on causation.

Difficulty: All five sentences discuss acts and omissions in philosophical vocabulary; the odd one can be seen only by tracking that the debate is about justifying the asymmetry through duties, not about causation. · Exams: CAT, XAT

CAT-VARC-C11-008 · Odd sentence out · MediumTITA
Five jumbled sentences (labelled 1, 2, 3, 4 and 5), related to a topic, are given below. Four of them can be put together to form a coherent paragraph. Identify the odd sentence and key in its number as your answer.

1.A teacher who wants to arouse curiosity might therefore begin not with a blank slate but with a partial picture and a puzzle it cannot yet explain.

2.Complete ignorance offers no visible gap, while complete knowledge leaves none, so interest is lowest at both extremes.

3.Knowing a little lets us see that there is a gap between what we know and what we could know, and the gap is what we want to close.

4.Curiosity is often imagined as strongest when we know nothing about a subject, but it seems to peak when we know a little.

5.Curiosity may also improve memory for incidental details that a person happens to meet while waiting for an answer they care about.

Type your answer
Answer and explanation

Answer: 5

Sentence 4 claims that curiosity peaks at partial knowledge, sentence 3 explains why in terms of a visible gap, sentence 2 applies the gap idea to the two extremes, and sentence 1 draws the teaching lesson ('therefore'). Sentence 5 is about curiosity, but it describes an effect of curiosity on memory, whereas the paragraph is about what causes curiosity; nothing in the gap argument leads to it or depends on it.

Common wrong entry 2: Sentence 2 may look repetitive, but it extends the gap explanation to both extremes, which is what justifies avoiding the 'blank slate' in sentence 1.

Common misconception: Accepting any claim about curiosity, and missing that the paragraph explains when curiosity arises, not what it does.

Difficulty: The odd sentence shares the subject and a learning context that seems to fit the teacher; it is rejected because it reverses the direction from cause to effect. · Exams: CAT, XAT, NMAT, SNAP, CMAT, MAH-CET

CAT-VARC-C11-009 · Odd sentence out · EasyTITA
Five jumbled sentences (labelled 1, 2, 3, 4 and 5), related to a topic, are given below. Four of them can be put together to form a coherent paragraph. Identify the odd sentence and key in its number as your answer.

1.Students without quiet rooms at home, job seekers without computers and elderly readers who want company all depend on that free, open space.

2.The earliest libraries kept clay tablets and scrolls, and they were often open only to priests, officials and scholars.

3.Judged by these uses, the library is less a warehouse of books than a piece of social infrastructure.

4.Public libraries are often discussed as if their only purpose were lending books, a function that cheap downloads seem to make obsolete.

5.In practice, many serve as one of the last indoor spaces where a person can stay for hours without being expected to buy anything.

Type your answer
Answer and explanation

Answer: 2

Sentence 4 states the common view that libraries exist to lend books, sentence 5 counters it with what libraries do 'in practice', sentence 1 lists who depends on 'that free, open space', and sentence 3 concludes from 'these uses'. Sentence 2 is about libraries, but it describes the distant past and restricted access, which has no place in an argument about the present role of public libraries.

Common wrong entry 4: Sentence 4 states a view the paragraph rejects, which can make it look odd, but the contrast 'In practice' in sentence 5 needs it.

Common misconception: Choosing a sentence on the same institution without noticing the shift of period and purpose.

Difficulty: The odd sentence moves to ancient history, an easily spotted change of time and scope. · Exams: CAT, XAT, NMAT, SNAP, CMAT, MAH-CET

CAT-VARC-C11-010 · Odd sentence out · MediumTITA
Five jumbled sentences (labelled 1, 2, 3, 4 and 5), related to a topic, are given below. Four of them can be put together to form a coherent paragraph. Identify the odd sentence and key in its number as your answer.

1.To issue money was to announce, in a form that passed through every hand, who now governed.

2.That is why rebels and usurpers often struck coins of their own almost as soon as they seized power, even before they could collect taxes.

3.In many ancient states, minting coins was as much a political act as an economic one.

4.Before coinage, many exchanges were settled in goods such as grain or cattle, whose value varied with the season and their condition.

5.A ruler's portrait or emblem stamped on every coin carried a claim of authority into markets far beyond the reach of his officials.

Type your answer
Answer and explanation

Answer: 4

Sentence 3 claims that minting was a political act, sentence 5 explains how a coin carried the ruler's claim of authority, sentence 2 draws on that ('That is why') to explain why usurpers minted at once, and sentence 1 states the general point. Sentence 4 is about money and exchange, but it describes what came before coinage and the economic drawback of goods as money, which says nothing about coins as political statements.

Common wrong entry 2: Sentence 2 can look like a narrow historical detail, but 'That is why' ties it to sentence 5, and it is the evidence that coins served authority even before taxation did.

Common misconception: Assuming that any sentence on the history of money belongs, when the paragraph argues about the political meaning of coins.

Difficulty: The odd sentence shares the theme of money; it is rejected because it makes an economic point about barter, not a political one about coins. · Exams: CAT, XAT, NMAT, SNAP, CMAT, MAH-CET

CAT-VARC-C11-011 · Odd sentence out · MediumTITA
Five jumbled sentences (labelled 1, 2, 3, 4 and 5), related to a topic, are given below. Four of them can be put together to form a coherent paragraph. Identify the odd sentence and key in its number as your answer.

1.Overbooking turns this predictable absence into income, at the price of occasionally having more passengers at the gate than seats.

2.Flying with those seats empty would waste revenue that cannot be recovered once the plane has left.

3.Fares also tend to rise as the departure date approaches, since travellers who book late are often less sensitive to price.

4.Many airlines sell more seats on a flight than the aircraft holds, because a predictable share of passengers fail to turn up.

5.The airline then pays compensation to volunteers who agree to take a later flight, and the policy pays as long as such payments cost less than the empty seats would have.

Type your answer
Answer and explanation

Answer: 3

Sentence 4 states the practice and its basis in no-shows, sentence 2 gives the cost of not overbooking ('those seats empty'), sentence 1 shows how overbooking recovers 'this predictable absence' at a risk, and sentence 5 explains how the risk is handled and when the policy pays. Sentence 3 is about airline revenue, but it describes how fares change over time, a pricing strategy unrelated to selling seats beyond capacity.

Common wrong entry 2: Sentence 2 can look obvious and removable, but 'those seats' refers to the no-shows of sentence 4, and the revenue it says would be lost is what sentence 1 says overbooking recovers.

Common misconception: Treating every airline revenue practice as belonging, when the paragraph explains a single one, overbooking.

Difficulty: The odd sentence shares the setting and the theme of revenue; it is rejected because its mechanism (pricing by booking date) is not the one under discussion. · Exams: CAT, XAT, NMAT, SNAP, CMAT, MAH-CET

CAT-VARC-C11-012 · Odd sentence out · EasyTITA
Five jumbled sentences (labelled 1, 2, 3, 4 and 5), related to a topic, are given below. Four of them can be put together to form a coherent paragraph. Identify the odd sentence and key in its number as your answer.

1.Tractors replaced draught animals on many farms during the twentieth century, greatly increasing the area one worker could cultivate.

2.For this reason some farmers have moved to reduced tillage, sowing seed directly into the residue of the previous crop.

3.But each pass also exposes organic matter to air, speeding its decay and releasing carbon that the soil had stored.

4.Ploughing breaks up the soil and buries weeds, which is why farmers have relied on it for thousands of years.

5.Repeated ploughing can therefore leave fields poorer in organic matter and more prone to erosion.

Type your answer
Answer and explanation

Answer: 1

Sentence 4 gives the benefits of ploughing, sentence 3 introduces its cost with 'But', sentence 5 states the consequence ('therefore') for the soil, and sentence 2 describes the response 'For this reason'. Sentence 1 is about farming, but it concerns the mechanisation of labour, not what ploughing does to the soil.

Common wrong entry 4: Sentence 4 praises ploughing while the paragraph criticises it, but the 'But' of sentence 3 needs the benefit it states.

Common misconception: Choosing a sentence because it is about farms, without checking whether it bears on soil organic matter.

Difficulty: The odd sentence turns to farm labour and machinery, an obvious change of focus. · Exams: CAT, XAT, NMAT, SNAP, CMAT, MAH-CET

CAT-VARC-C11-013 · Odd sentence out · MediumTITA
Five jumbled sentences (labelled 1, 2, 3, 4 and 5), related to a topic, are given below. Four of them can be put together to form a coherent paragraph. Identify the odd sentence and key in its number as your answer.

1.Most of what any of us believes, from the shape of the Earth to the date of our own birth, rests on the word of others rather than on our own observation.

2.Yet trust in testimony need not be blind, for we can ask whether a source has been reliable before and whether it has any motive to deceive.

3.If knowledge required checking everything for ourselves, we would know almost nothing.

4.Testimony is thus a genuine source of knowledge, but one that leaves us with the work of deciding whom to believe.

5.In a courtroom, testimony is usually given under oath, and lying under oath is itself a punishable offence.

Type your answer
Answer and explanation

Answer: 5

Sentence 1 observes that most belief rests on the word of others, sentence 3 shows that knowledge could not do without it, sentence 2 answers the worry that such trust is blind, and sentence 4 concludes ('thus') that testimony yields knowledge but demands judgement. Sentence 5 uses the word testimony in its legal sense and describes court procedure, which plays no part in an argument about how ordinary belief can rest on others' word.

Common wrong entry 3: Sentence 3 may look too short to matter, but it is the reason sentence 1 matters: without it there is no case that testimony must count as knowledge.

Common misconception: Linking the oath to the 'motive to deceive' in sentence 2 and so treating a point of legal procedure as part of a general argument about knowledge.

Difficulty: The odd sentence shares the key term and could seem to illustrate a motive not to deceive; it must be rejected because it shifts from the epistemology of belief to court procedure. · Exams: CAT, XAT, NMAT, SNAP, CMAT, MAH-CET

CAT-VARC-C11-014 · Odd sentence out · EasyTITA
Five jumbled sentences (labelled 1, 2, 3, 4 and 5), related to a topic, are given below. Four of them can be put together to form a coherent paragraph. Identify the odd sentence and key in its number as your answer.

1.Uncertainty stretches waiting further, so a delay of unknown length can feel worse than a longer one that has been announced in advance.

2.This is why some buildings place mirrors beside their lifts, giving people something to look at while they wait.

3.Waiting for a reward can also be a test of self-control, as when a child is asked to hold off eating one sweet in order to earn two.

4.Time spent waiting with nothing to do feels longer than the same time spent occupied.

5.Those who manage waiting rooms and counters can therefore make waits more bearable without shortening them, simply by filling the time and telling people how long it will last.

Type your answer
Answer and explanation

Answer: 3

Sentence 4 states that idle waiting feels longer, sentence 2 gives a practice explained by that ('This is why'), sentence 1 adds a second factor, uncertainty, and sentence 5 draws the lesson ('therefore') from both, filling the time and announcing its length. Sentence 3 is about waiting, but it treats waiting as a test of self-control, not as an experience whose felt length can be managed, so it connects to none of the other sentences.

Common wrong entry 1: Sentence 1 introduces a new factor and can look like a change of subject, but sentence 5 needs it: 'telling people how long it will last' is the remedy for uncertainty.

Common misconception: Choosing a sentence on the same word, waiting, without checking that it concerns how long a wait feels.

Difficulty: The odd sentence uses the paragraph's key word in a different sense, and the paragraph's own chain of reasons is explicit. · Exams: CAT, XAT, NMAT, SNAP, CMAT, MAH-CET

CAT-VARC-C11-015 · Odd sentence out · DifficultTITA
Five jumbled sentences (labelled 1, 2, 3, 4 and 5), related to a topic, are given below. Four of them can be put together to form a coherent paragraph. Identify the odd sentence and key in its number as your answer.

1.Writing, by contrast, allowed a poem to survive the death of every singer who knew it.

2.It was once assumed that singers of long oral epics must have memorised their poems word for word, as a modern actor learns a script.

3.What he means by sameness is fidelity to the story and its traditional manner, not repetition of a fixed text, a notion that belongs to cultures of writing.

4.Closer study of living traditions suggested something different: singers rebuild each performance from a stock of formulaic phrases and familiar scenes.

5.Two performances of the 'same' epic by one singer may therefore differ in hundreds of lines, while the singer insists, sincerely, that he has sung it exactly as before.

Type your answer
Answer and explanation

Answer: 1

Sentence 2 reports the old assumption of word-for-word memory, sentence 4 corrects it with the finding that singers rebuild each performance, sentence 5 draws the consequence ('therefore') that two performances differ though the singer calls them the same, and sentence 3 explains what 'sameness' means to him. Sentence 1 picks up the word 'writing' from sentence 3, but it turns to the survival of poems, a question of preservation; 'by contrast' sets writing against oral tradition, but on a point, survival, that the paragraph never raises; the paragraph is about what counts as the same poem in an oral tradition, and no sentence builds on or leads to a claim about preservation.

Common wrong entry 2: Sentence 2 states a view the paragraph rejects, which tempts students to drop it, but 'something different' in sentence 4 needs it, and the actor's script is the model of sameness that sentence 3 sets aside.
Common wrong entry 3: Sentence 3 can look like a philosophical add-on, but it resolves the puzzle of sentence 5: why the singer can sincerely call two different performances the same.

Common misconception: Attaching sentence 1 to the end of sentence 3 because both mention writing, without noticing that the paragraph never discusses whether poems survive.

Difficulty: The odd sentence borrows a key word and a contrastive connective that seem to continue the last sentence; it is rejected only by seeing that its topic, preservation, is not the paragraph's topic, sameness. · Exams: CAT, XAT

CAT-VARC-C11-016 · Odd sentence out · MediumTITA
Five jumbled sentences (labelled 1, 2, 3, 4 and 5), related to a topic, are given below. Four of them can be put together to form a coherent paragraph. Identify the odd sentence and key in its number as your answer.

1.Many constitutions also set a minimum age for high office, on the view that maturity should come before power.

2.The cost is that voters lose the option of keeping a leader they still want, and the experience she has gained leaves with her.

3.A leader who knows she must leave has less reason to capture courts or electoral bodies, since she will not be there to benefit.

4.Whether term limits are wise therefore depends on how real the danger of entrenchment is in a given country, set against the value of that lost choice and experience.

5.Limits on how long a leader may hold office are meant to prevent the slow entrenchment of power, in which an incumbent bends institutions in order to keep winning.

Type your answer
Answer and explanation

Answer: 1

Sentence 5 states the purpose of term limits, sentence 3 explains how they work against entrenchment, sentence 2 states 'The cost', and sentence 4 weighs the benefit against 'that lost choice and experience'. Sentence 1 concerns another constitutional rule about who may hold office, but an age requirement has nothing to do with entrenchment or the cost of forcing leaders out, so it plays no part in the trade-off.

Common wrong entry 2: Sentence 2 runs against the case for term limits, but it is needed: sentence 4 weighs the danger of entrenchment against exactly the cost it states.

Common misconception: Treating any constitutional limit on office as part of the argument, when the paragraph weighs one rule only.

Difficulty: The odd sentence shares the constitutional vocabulary and opens with 'also'; it is rejected because the paragraph is a cost and benefit analysis of term limits alone. · Exams: CAT, XAT, NMAT, SNAP, CMAT, MAH-CET

CAT-VARC-C11-017 · Odd sentence out · EasyTITA
Five jumbled sentences (labelled 1, 2, 3, 4 and 5), related to a topic, are given below. Four of them can be put together to form a coherent paragraph. Identify the odd sentence and key in its number as your answer.

1.What keeps the arrangement from collapsing is social pressure: members are usually neighbours or colleagues who would lose face by defaulting after their turn.

2.Many banks now offer mobile applications that let customers open an account without ever visiting a branch.

3.The member who receives it first gets, in effect, an interest-free loan, while the one who receives it last has simply saved.

4.Such groups thus let people without access to banks save and borrow, using reputation in place of collateral.

5.In a rotating savings group, each member contributes a fixed sum every month, and each month one member takes the whole pot.

Type your answer
Answer and explanation

Answer: 2

Sentence 5 describes how a rotating savings group works, sentence 3 shows that it acts as a loan for early recipients and as saving for late ones, sentence 1 explains what prevents default, and sentence 4 concludes ('thus') what such groups achieve. Sentence 2 is about finance, but it describes banking technology, which has nothing to do with the informal mechanism the paragraph analyses.

Common wrong entry 1: Sentence 1 can look like a remark about social life, but it explains why the early recipient of sentence 3 does not run off, and it supplies the 'reputation' of sentence 4.

Common misconception: Choosing a sentence because it seems to contrast with banks, without seeing that the paragraph is about how the informal group itself works.

Difficulty: The odd sentence turns to formal banking technology, an evident change of focus. · Exams: CAT, XAT, NMAT, SNAP, CMAT, MAH-CET

CAT-VARC-C11-018 · Odd sentence out · DifficultTITA
Five jumbled sentences (labelled 1, 2, 3, 4 and 5), related to a topic, are given below. Four of them can be put together to form a coherent paragraph. Identify the odd sentence and key in its number as your answer.

1.A surveyor stepping along it with a long rod skips every inlet smaller than the rod, while one using a short rod follows them and records a greater total.

2.The sensible question is therefore not how long a coast is, but how its measured length changes with the scale of measurement, a property that can be compared between coasts.

3.Coastlines also change over time, as waves wear away headlands and rivers build new land at their mouths.

4.The length of a rugged coastline is not a fixed quantity waiting to be measured; it depends on the length of the ruler used to measure it.

5.As the ruler shrinks, the measured length keeps growing, because ever smaller bays and rocks come into the count.

Type your answer
Answer and explanation

Answer: 3

Sentence 4 claims that a coast's length depends on the ruler, sentence 1 shows why with the long and short rods, sentence 5 generalises to an ever shrinking ruler, and sentence 2 concludes ('therefore') that the useful quantity is how length changes with scale. Sentence 3 also suggests that a coast's length is not fixed, but for a different reason: the coast itself changes. The paragraph's argument holds for a coast frozen in time, since it is about measurement, so sentence 3 supports none of its steps.

Common wrong entry 5: Sentence 5 can look like a repetition of sentence 1, but it turns two rods into a trend ('keeps growing') without which the conclusion about how length changes with scale has no basis.
Common wrong entry 2: Sentence 2 may look like a change of subject from measuring to comparing, but it is the conclusion the paragraph has been building towards.

Common misconception: Taking sentence 3 as support for 'not a fixed quantity' in sentence 4, and missing that sentence 4 at once names its reason, the length of the ruler.

Difficulty: The odd sentence seems to support the opening claim that length is not fixed; rejecting it needs the distinction between a coast that changes and a measurement that depends on scale. · Exams: CAT, XAT

Common wrong entry 2: Sentence 2 can look like an abstract aside, but it is the alternative that sentence 5 draws its conclusion from; without it the paragraph criticises a theory and offers nothing in its place.

CAT-VARC-C11-054 · Odd sentence out · DifficultTITA

1.Because the number comes from the patient alone, it can also be deliberately inflated, and for this reason some clinicians prefer to disregard it altogether.

2.The scale is therefore most trustworthy when it is used to follow a single patient over time, since the same person is likely to keep roughly the same anchors from one day to the next.

3.When a clinician asks a patient to score pain from nought to ten, the result is a number, and numbers carry an air of objectivity that a description lacks.

4.Yet each patient anchors the scale to their own history, so that a seven from someone who has never been badly hurt may mean something quite different from a seven from someone who has.

5.Comparing scores across different patients, by contrast, risks treating a private yardstick as though it were a shared one.

Type your answer
Answer and explanation

Answer: 1

The four sentences build one argument: a pain score looks objective (3), but it is anchored to personal history (4), so it is reliable within one patient over time (2) and unreliable across patients (5, 'by contrast'). Sentence 1 also criticises the score, which makes it tempting, but it raises a different problem (deliberate exaggeration rather than personal anchoring) and ends by recommending that the score be disregarded 'altogether'. That dismissal cuts against sentence 2, which holds that the scale is 'most trustworthy' for tracking one patient, and nothing in the argument about anchoring builds on the worry about exaggeration, so the paragraph is complete without it.

Common wrong entry 5: Sentence 5 may look like an afterthought, but 'by contrast' completes the contrast begun in sentence 2: reliable within one patient, unreliable across patients.
Common wrong entry 3: Sentence 3 may seem neutral, but its 'air of objectivity' is exactly what sentence 4 begins to undercut with 'Yet'.

Common misconception: Accepting sentence 1 because it, like sentence 4, doubts the pain score, without noticing that its remedy contradicts the paragraph's conclusion.

Difficulty: The odd sentence shares the topic, the critical tone and the vocabulary of the paragraph; it is rejected only because its kind of objection and its conclusion clash with the argument about anchoring. · Exams: CAT, XAT

3.Handwriting analysis is sometimes offered as a way of judging a person's character, though there is little good evidence that it works.

4.The disagreement is less about nostalgia than about a real question: whether the hand teaches the mind something that the keyboard does not.

5.As more writing is done on keyboards, many schools have reduced the time they spend teaching children to write by hand.

Type your answer
Answer and explanation

Answer: 3

Sentence 5 describes the change in schools, sentences 1 and 2 give the two sides ('Some teachers' and 'Others'), and sentence 4 names what 'The disagreement' is really about. Sentence 3 is about handwriting, but it concerns reading character from it; the paragraph is about whether children should be taught to write by hand, and the claims of handwriting analysis play no part in that debate.

Common wrong entry 4: Sentence 4 may seem to step back from the debate, but 'The disagreement' refers to the two sides in sentences 1 and 2, and it is the paragraph's conclusion.

Common misconception: Accepting sentence 3 because it, like sentence 1, links handwriting to the mind.

Difficulty: The odd sentence shares the subject and a link between handwriting and the mind, so it is rejected on function within the debate about teaching. · Exams: CAT, XAT, NMAT, SNAP, CMAT, MAH-CET

CAT-VARC-C11-073 · Odd sentence out · DifficultTITA
Five jumbled sentences (labelled 1, 2, 3, 4 and 5), related to a topic, are given below. Four of them can be put together to form a coherent paragraph. Identify the odd sentence and key in its number as your answer.

1.An archive is not a neutral sample of the past but a deposit of what particular institutions thought worth writing down and keeping.

2.Many archives have also suffered losses through fire, flood and neglect, so that some records once carefully kept now survive only in fragments.

3.Historians have learned to read such records against their purpose, finding in a list of debts or a trial transcript glimpses of lives the clerks never meant to describe.

4.Even so, there are limits to what this method can recover, and where the documents fall silent the honest course is to mark the gap rather than fill it with conjecture.

5.The resulting silences are not random: a tax office records who owed what, a court records who was accused of what, and people who neither paid much nor offended anyone may leave hardly a trace.

Type your answer
Answer and explanation

Answer: 2

The paragraph argues that archival silence reflects what institutions chose to record (1), that these silences fall on particular kinds of people (5, 'not random'), that historians answer this bias by reading records 'against their purpose' (3), and that 'this method' has limits (4). Sentence 2 also concerns gaps in archives, which makes it tempting, but it describes accidental destruction, the kind of loss that sentence 5 sets aside by calling the silences 'not random'. Reading against the purpose of a record is a reply to what clerks chose to note, not to what fire destroyed, so sentence 2 does not connect to the method the paragraph goes on to discuss.

Common wrong entry 4: Sentence 4 may look as if it moves to a separate point about honesty, but 'this method' refers to reading records 'against their purpose' in sentence 3, and the paragraph needs this limit as its qualification.
Common wrong entry 5: Sentence 5 may look like a list of examples that could be dropped, but 'such records' in sentence 3 refers to its tax and court records, and its claim that the silences are 'not random' carries the argument of sentence 1 forward.

Common misconception: Accepting sentence 2 because it, like sentence 4, is about gaps in the record, without noticing that the paragraph is about gaps produced by selection rather than by accident.

Difficulty: The odd sentence shares the theme of gaps in the record and the vocabulary of archives; it is rejected only by seeing that the paragraph's silences are chosen, not accidental, and that the method it describes answers selection alone. · Exams: CAT, XAT

3.Winning therefore carries bad news: it tells the winner that it was probably the most optimistic bidder in the room, not the most perceptive.

4.The firm that wins is the one whose estimate was highest, and the highest of many scattered estimates is likely to be too high.

5.Oil companies also face the danger that prices will fall between the auction and the first barrel, a risk that no estimate of the reserves can remove.

Type your answer
Answer and explanation

Answer: 5

Sentence 1 sets up scattered estimates of a single true value, sentence 4 notes that the winner holds the highest estimate, which is likely to be too high, sentence 3 draws the consequence ('therefore') that winning is bad news, and sentence 2 gives the rational response, bidding below one's estimate, the more so as rivals multiply. Sentence 5 shares the oilfield, the auction and even the danger of overpaying, but the risk it describes, a fall in prices, has nothing to do with the statistical pitfall of being the most optimistic estimator, and nothing in sentence 2 answers it.

Common wrong entry 2: Sentence 2 can look like advice tacked on, but 'this' refers to the bad news of sentence 3, and 'the more rivals' follows from the logic of sentence 4: more estimates make the highest one more likely to be too high.
Common wrong entry 1: Sentence 1 is essential because the idea that estimates 'scatter around the true amount' is what makes the highest estimate likely to be too high in sentence 4.

Common misconception: Picking a coherent sentence because sentence 5 also describes a way in which the winning firm can lose money, and so seems to belong to the same argument.

Difficulty: The odd sentence shares the setting and the theme of overpaying; separating it requires seeing that the paragraph is about the selection effect of winning, not market risk in general. · Exams: CAT, XAT

Answer and explanation

Sentence 1 also complicates eradication, which makes it tempting, but it is about a hazard of the method, poison reaching native birds during the operation. 4.Some economists hold that benefits in the future should count for less than benefits now, simply because they lie further ahead in time.

5.The argument proves too much, however: someone who buried a device set to explode in a hundred years would plainly be doing wrong, though every eventual victim is yet unborn.

Type your answer
Answer and explanation

Answer: 4

Sentence 3 states the argument that we owe nothing to future people, sentence 5 refutes it with the buried device, sentence 1 identifies what makes that act wrong, and sentence 2 extends the principle to real policy. Sentence 4 is about the future, but it asks how much future benefits should weigh, not whether obligations to people not yet born can exist at all, so it does not belong.

Common wrong entry 5: Sentence 5 reads like a vivid digression, but 'proves too much' marks it as the refutation that sentence 1 then explains.

Common misconception: Linking sentence 4 to sentence 2 because both concern policies with distant effects, though discounting is a different question from whether future people can be owed anything.

Difficulty: The odd sentence comes from the same debate about the future and could pass as an objection, so the solver must see that the paragraph's question is existence, not weight. · Exams: CAT, XAT

CAT-VARC-C11-179 · Odd sentence out · DifficultTITA
Five jumbled sentences (labelled 1, 2, 3, 4 and 5), related to a topic, are given below. Four of them can be put together to form a coherent paragraph. Identify the odd sentence and key in its number as your answer.

1.When a town builds an embankment along its river, the land behind it suddenly looks safe enough for houses, shops and schools.

2.The embankment works as designed, holding back the ordinary floods of most years, but when a rare flood overtops it the damage exceeds anything the unprotected town could have suffered.

3.Development follows, and within a generation far more property stands on the floodplain than stood there before the defence was raised.

4.This is why some planners argue that keeping floodplains as parks or farmland, though it looks like a waste of valuable land, is the cheaper form of protection.

5.Earth embankments also fail far more often than their designers expect, because seepage weakens them long before the river reaches its peak.

Type your answer
Answer and explanation

Answer: 5

The paragraph's argument is behavioural: the embankment makes land look safe (sentence 1), development follows (sentence 3), and so a defence that 'works as designed' still raises losses when a rare flood overtops it (sentence 2); 'This is why' in sentence 4 draws the planning conclusion. Sentence 5 attacks embankments on engineering grounds, claiming they often fail, which contradicts the concession in sentence 2 that the embankment works as designed and replaces the paragraph's mechanism (more property exposed) with a different one (structural failure).

Common wrong entry 2: Sentence 2 concedes that embankments work, which can look out of place in a critical paragraph, but it is the hinge of the argument: the losses grow even when the defence performs, and 'This is why' in sentence 4 refers to it.
Common wrong entry 4: Sentence 4 introduces planners, but it is the conclusion the whole chain leads to, and without it the paragraph has no point.

Common misconception: Choosing the concession in sentence 2 because it seems to defend embankments, or keeping sentence 5 because it also sounds critical of embankments.

Difficulty: The odd sentence shares the paragraph's scepticism about embankments, so it must be rejected because it changes the mechanism and clashes with the concession. · Exams: CAT, XAT

CAT-VARC-C11-194 · Odd sentence out · DifficultTITA
Five jumbled sentences (labelled 1, 2, 3, 4 and 5), related to a topic, are given below. Four of them can be put together to form a coherent paragraph. Identify the odd sentence and key in its number as your answer.

1.Maps are usually thought of as describing territory, but in the age of empires they often helped to create it.

2.Yet the power of maps cut both ways, for a line once published could be cited against the very state that drew it when that state later wished to move it.

3.Areas left blank were read not as places unknown to the mapmaker but as places awaiting an owner, which made them seem open to claim.

4.Improvements in surveying instruments made maps steadily more accurate, so that later maps of a region often corrected the outlines of earlier ones.

5.A boundary drawn across land that its surveyors had barely seen became, once printed, the line along which taxes were collected and soldiers posted.

Type your answer
Answer and explanation

Answer: 4

Sentence 1 states the thesis that imperial maps created territory, sentences 5 and 3 give two ways they did so (printed boundaries became administrative fact; blank spaces invited claims), and sentence 2 ('Yet the power of maps cut both ways') adds that a published line could also bind its maker. Sentence 4 is about maps, but it tells a story of growing accuracy, that is, of maps getting better at describing, which is the view sentence 1 sets aside; it contributes nothing to the argument about maps shaping power.

Common wrong entry 2: Sentence 2 begins 'Yet' and seems to undercut the thesis, but it extends it: the power of maps to fix territory is what lets a line be 'cited against the very state that drew it'.
Common wrong entry 3: Sentence 3 can look like a separate point about blank spaces, but it is the second way, after sentence 5, in which maps created territory.

Common misconception: Keeping sentence 4 because correcting old outlines seems related to moving a line in sentence 2, or dropping sentence 2 because it reverses the direction of the argument.

Difficulty: The odd sentence shares the vocabulary of lines and outlines and seems to echo the sentence about moving a published line; it is rejected only by seeing that it treats maps as description, the view the paragraph moves beyond. · Exams: CAT, XAT

Common misconception: Keeping sentence 1 because it seems to explain why firms 'put off revisions' in sentence 2, when sentence 2 already gives the reason: revisions are costly.

Answer and explanation

Answer: 3

Sentences 1, 5, 2 and 4 explain one feature of memory: the worst moment and the ending dominate, length matters little 'by contrast', 'As a result' a longer procedure can be remembered as better, and 'in other words' memory is not a faithful summary. Sentence 3 mentions hospitals and patients, but it is about a comfort offered while waiting, not about how an experience is remembered.

Common wrong entry 5: Sentence 5 can look like a minor aside, but the claim that length matters little is what makes the surprising result in sentence 2 possible.

Common misconception: Choosing sentence 4 because it generalises beyond medicine, when 'in other words' marks it as the conclusion drawn from sentence 2.

Difficulty: The odd sentence shares only the hospital setting and is easily separated from the argument about memory. · Exams: CAT, XAT, NMAT, SNAP, CMAT, MAH-CET

CAT-VARC-C11-245 · Odd sentence out · MediumTITA
Five jumbled sentences (labelled 1, 2, 3, 4 and 5), related to a topic, are given below. Four of them can be put together to form a coherent paragraph. Identify the odd sentence and key in its number as your answer.

1.Turning to something easier brings immediate relief, and that relief is what makes the habit so hard to break.

2.The habit of putting tasks off is commonly blamed on disorganisation, as though a sharper timetable would be enough to cure it.

3.Deadlines set by someone else are often met more reliably than the ones people set for themselves.

4.On this view, procrastination is a way of handling feelings rather than a lapse in planning, so remedies that ignore emotion are likely to fail.

5.Yet those who delay a piece of work can usually say exactly how long it will take; what they shrink from is the discomfort it stirs up.

Type your answer
Answer and explanation

Answer: 3

The paragraph replaces one account with another: sentence 2 states the usual view, sentence 5 objects with 'Yet' that what is avoided is discomfort, not lost track of time, sentence 1 explains how 'that relief' keeps the habit going, and sentence 4 concludes 'On this view' that the problem is one of handling feelings. Sentence 3 uses the language of deadlines and scheduling, but it belongs to the time-management account that the paragraph sets aside, and none of the other sentences builds on it.

Common wrong entry 2: Sentence 2 gives a view the author rejects, but it is what 'Yet' in sentence 5 answers and what sentence 4 finally replaces.

Common misconception: Keeping sentence 3 because it is about putting things off, without noticing that it treats delay as a matter of deadlines, the view sentence 5 rejects.

Difficulty: The odd sentence is on the same subject and sounds like practical advice, so it must be rejected because it belongs to the view the paragraph argues against. · Exams: CAT, XAT, NMAT, SNAP, CMAT, MAH-CET

CAT-VARC-C11-246 · Odd sentence out · DifficultTITA

1.Asked to explain the mechanism step by step, however, many discover that their understanding was far thinner than they had believed.

2.Explaining a subject to someone else is also one of the most effective ways of learning it thoroughly.

3.The confidence seems to come from long familiarity with using the object, which is easily mistaken for knowledge of how it works.

4.Most people feel confident that they understand how familiar devices work, such as a zip or a flush toilet.

5.The same confusion may be at work in public debate, where people often hold firm opinions about policies whose workings they could not explain if asked.

Type your answer
Answer and explanation

Answer: 2

Sentences 4, 1, 3 and 5 trace one illusion: people feel they understand familiar devices, attempting an explanation 'however' exposes how thin that understanding is, 'The confidence' is traced to familiarity with use, and 'The same confusion' is extended to opinions about policy. Sentence 2 shares the idea of explaining, but it praises explanation as a way of learning, a point about study technique that does not concern the gap between felt and real understanding.

Common wrong entry 5: Sentence 5 moves from devices to public debate, but 'The same confusion' refers to the mistake described in sentence 3, so it extends the argument rather than leaving it.
Common wrong entry 3: Sentence 3 might look like an optional aside, yet without it 'The same confusion' in sentence 5 would have nothing to refer to.

Common misconception: Keeping sentence 2 because it seems to follow from the step-by-step explanation in sentence 1, without seeing that in the paragraph explaining is a test that reveals an illusion, not a method of learning.

Difficulty: The odd sentence echoes the paragraph's key activity, explaining, so it must be rejected on function; the sentence extending the point to public debate also moves to a new domain and tempts as the odd one. · Exams: CAT, XAT

3.Freedom from a rule, it seems, does not always mean freedom from the pressure that the rule once absorbed.

5.Some professions, such as medicine and aviation, keep uniforms partly so that the public can recognise at a glance who is responsible.

Answer and explanation

Answer: 5

The four linked sentences describe an unexpected result: sentence 1 gives the expectation, sentence 2 reports with 'Instead' that the change made choosing harder, sentence 4 explains why the old rule was easier, and sentence 3 draws the general lesson about 'Freedom from a rule'. Sentence 5 is about uniforms at work, but its point is that uniforms help the public identify staff, which has nothing to do with the anxiety of employees choosing clothes.

Common wrong entry 4: Sentence 4 can look like a detour into fashion, but it gives the reason behind sentence 2: the old rule supplied 'a safe answer', and its absence leaves unwritten standards.

Common misconception: Choosing sentence 4 because it compares old and new styles in detail, when it explains the difficulty reported in sentence 2.

Difficulty: The odd sentence shares the vocabulary of workplace clothing, so it must be rejected because it serves a different purpose (public recognition) rather than the employees' experience. · Exams: CAT, XAT, NMAT, SNAP, CMAT, MAH-CET

2.Popular Indian films have long been made with an interval in the middle, a pause during which audiences stretch, talk and buy snacks.

3.When such films are streamed without a break, that carefully placed turn can lose much of its force.

4.Multiplex cinemas, with several small screens under one roof, have allowed halls to show a wider range of films to smaller audiences.

5.The interval thus shapes the structure of the film itself, dividing it into two halves with distinct rhythms.

Type your answer
Answer and explanation

Answer: 4

Sentences 2, 1, 5 and 3 follow the interval from custom to craft: films include a break, film-makers build 'this break' into the story, the interval 'thus' shapes the film's structure, and without it 'that carefully placed turn' weakens. Sentence 4 is about cinema halls, but it concerns how multiplexes changed what is screened, not the place of the interval in a film's construction.

Common wrong entry 3: Sentence 3 shifts to streaming, but 'that carefully placed turn' refers to the dramatic turn of sentence 1, so it completes the argument by showing what is lost without the break.

Common misconception: Choosing sentence 3 because it moves from cinema halls to streaming, when 'that carefully placed turn' links it to sentence 1 and it draws the consequence of the argument.

Difficulty: The odd sentence shares the cinema setting, so it must be rejected because it concerns film exhibition rather than how the interval shapes a story. · Exams: CAT, XAT, NMAT, SNAP, CMAT, MAH-CET

CAT-VARC-C11-250 · Odd sentence out · DifficultTITA
Five jumbled sentences (labelled 1, 2, 3, 4 and 5), related to a topic, are given below. Four of them can be put together to form a coherent paragraph. Identify the odd sentence and key in its number as your answer.

1.Its deeper effect is on the relation between host and guest: since everyone has contributed, no one leaves owing a return invitation.

2.This is also why a potluck tolerates wildly uneven contributions, a lavish curry beside a packet of crisps, without embarrassment: what is being shared is participation, not an exact division of costs.

3.The meal becomes a joint production rather than a gift from one household to the others.

4.Because guests cook without consulting one another, a potluck can easily end up with three rice dishes and no dessert.

5.A potluck, in which each guest brings a dish to share, looks at first like a simple way of spreading the cost of a dinner.

Type your answer
Answer and explanation

Answer: 4

The paragraph moves from an apparent purpose to a deeper one: sentence 5 gives the cost-sharing view, sentence 1 says the 'deeper effect' is on the host-guest relation, sentence 3 restates it as a 'joint production', and sentence 2 uses that idea to explain why uneven contributions cause no embarrassment, returning to the 'division of costs' of sentence 5. Sentence 4 is about potlucks too, but it describes a practical problem of coordination, which plays no part in the argument about obligation and participation.

Common wrong entry 3: Sentence 3 can look like a mere restatement of sentence 1, but 'joint production' is the idea that 'This is also why' in sentence 2 relies on.
Common wrong entry 5: Sentence 5 gives a view the paragraph goes beyond, yet 'Its deeper effect' in sentence 1 needs it, and sentence 2 returns to its talk of costs.

Common misconception: Keeping sentence 4 because it seems to belong with the mixed dishes of sentence 2, although sentence 2 concerns uneven generosity, not lack of planning.

Difficulty: The odd sentence is a lively detail on the same subject and sits naturally beside the mention of uneven contributions, so it must be rejected on function. · Exams: CAT, XAT
